                                              ARITHMETIC DUALITY FOR TWO-DIMENSIONAL LOCAL
                                                    RINGS WITH PERFECT RESIDUE FIELD
arXiv:2112.06491v4 [math.NT] 13 May 2023




                                                                                TAKASHI SUZUKI


                                                    Abstract. We give a refinement of Saito’s arithmetic duality for two-dimensional
                                                    local rings by giving algebraic group structures for arithmetic cohomology
                                                    groups.

                                  1. Introduction
1.1. Aim of the paper. Let A be a complete noetherian normal two-dimensional
local ring with perfect residue field F of characteristic p > 0. When F is finite, Saito
[Sai86, Sai87] gives arithmetic duality and class field theory for A by combining
Kato’s two-dimensional local class field theories [Kat79] for local fields at all height
one prime ideals of A. On the other hand, Serre [Ser61] and Hazewinkel [DG70,
Appendix] refine local class field theory for one-dimensional local fields by allowing
arbitrary perfect residue fields and giving pro-algebraic group structures for unit
groups. In [Suz22b], we give a functorial reformulation of Serre-Hazewinkel’s theory
by using the so-called “rational étale site”.
   In this paper, we refine Saito’s theory in the style of Serre and Hazewinkel,
by allowing arbitrary perfect F and giving ind-pro-algebraic group structures for
arithmetic cohomology groups attached to A, in the case where A has mixed char-
acteristic. We use the formalism of the ind-rational pro-étale site [Suz20b] and the
perfect artinian étale site [Suz21], which are improvements of the rational étale site.
   Our results are arithmetic in nature but, at the same time, also purely algebro-
geometric, since the residue field is a completely general perfect field. They also
                        ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                     3


reflect the intricacy of the singularity of A and its resolutions. For these reasons,
we hope that our work will be of interest for not only number theorists but also
pure algebraic geometers.
   However, we have to admit that the results of this paper are not in the best
possible form. Saito’s theory treats the divisor class group of A, the Brauer group
of the punctured spectrum, K2 -idèle class groups, unramified or ramified abelian
extensions of the fraction field, homology of graphs of resolutions and Hasse princi-
ples. Compared to this rich theory, our theory is more abstract and not so complete,
since it is still on the level of cohomology groups. Also, we do not give finiteness
statements in the ideal strongest form. Deducing more concrete consequences in
Saito’s style from our theory as well as appropriate finiteness statements is the
subject of our next papers [Suz22c] and [Suz22a].
1.2. Notation. We need some notation to state our results. Let F a perfect field
of characteristic p > 0. Let Algu /F be the category of perfections (inverse limits
along Frobenius morphisms) of commutative unipotent algebraic groups over F
with group scheme morphisms ([Ser60]). It is an abelian category. Let PAlgu /F
and IAlgu /F be its pro-category and ind-category, respectively, and IPAlgu /F the
ind-category of PAlgu /F . Let Db (IPAlgu /F ) be the bounded derived category of
IPAlgu /F .
    An F -algebra is said to be rational if it can be written as a finite product F1′ ×· · ·×
Fn′ , where each Fi′ is the perfection of a finitely generated field over F ([Suz22b]).
Let F rat be the category of rational F -algebras with F -algebra homomorphisms.
Let F indrat be the ind-category of F rat (viewed as a full subcategory of the category

ported cohomology of U have canonical structures as objects of IPAlgu /F :

Theorem 1.3.1.
     (1) There exist canonical objects

               RΓ(U, Z/pn Z(r)), RΓc (U, Z/pn Z(r)) ∈ Db (IPAlgu /F )

         homology objects of RΓ(U, Z/pn Z(r)) and RΓc (U, Z/pn Z(r)), respectively.
            Then the base changes of Hq (U, Z/pn Z(r)) and Hqc (U, Z/pn Z(r)) to F
         with the natural Gal(F /F )-equivariant structures are canonically isomor-
         phic to Hq (U, Z/pn Z(r)) and Hqc (U, Z/pn Z(r)), respectively.
     (3) In particular, for any q ∈ Z, we have

    We will prove the following refinement of Saito’s arithmetic duality [Sai86, Sai87]:

Theorem 1.3.2. Set r′ = 2 − r. Then there exists a canonical morphism

               RΓ(U, Z/pn Z(r)) ⊗L RΓc (U, Z/pn Z(r′ )) → Qp /Zp [−3]

   The group H 1 (U, Z/pn Z) classifies abelian p-coverings of U . We have an exact
sequence

            0 → Pic(U )/pn Pic(U ) → H 2 (U, Z/pn Z(1)) → Br(U )[pn ] → 0.

The group Hc3 (U, Z/pn Z(2)) contains the K2 -idèle class group of U ([Sai87, §1])
mod pn . The above theorems thus give ind-pro-algebraic group structures for these
important groups and show their duality.

1.4. Ideas of proof. The basic idea lies in Saito’s computations [Sai86, (4.11) (A),
(B)] of his duality pairing when F is finite. He introduces a filtration on K1 and
K2 by symbols and writes the induced pairings on the graded pieces as the pairing
                                               residue   TrF/Fp
                    F [[t]] × (Ω1F ((t)) /Ω1F [[t]] ) −→ F −→ Z/pZ

and similar pairings with F [[t]]/F [[t]]p , F ((t))× /F ((t))×p
                                                             Q , F ((t))/(Frob − 1)F ((t))
and so on. Now we view F [[t]] as a pro-algebraic group n≥0 Ga tn and Ω1F ((t)) /Ω1F [[t]]
as an ind-algebraic group n≥1 Ga t−n dt. We replace the trace map F → Z/pZ by
                            L

the morphism Ga → Z/pZ[1] to the shift of Z/pZ in the derived category coming
from the Artin-Schreier extension class 0 → Z/pZ → Ga → Ga → 0. The multi-
plication morphism Ga × Ga → Ga followed by the morphism Ga → Z/pZ[1] is a
perfect pairing in a suitable sense.
   How do we perform such replacement for the entire K1 and K2 and cohomol-
ogy groups in a canonical manner? For a perfect (possibly transcendental) field
extension F ′ /F , define the “base change”, “F ′ ⊗F A”, by
                                  ˆ W (F ) A = lim (W (F ′ ) ⊗W (F ) A/mn ),
               A(F ′ ) = W (F ′ ) ⊗
                                               ←−
                                              n≥1

where W denotes the p-typical Witt vectors of infinite length and m the maximal
ideal of A. This ring A(F ′ ) is the same kind of object as A, except that the
residue field is now F ′ . Now we can run Saito’s theory for the ring A(F ′ ) instead
of A. Then everything becomes a functor in F ′ . The group F [[t]] above is now the
functor F ′ 7→ F ′ [[t]]. By the theory of the perfect artinian étale site, such functors
in perfect field extensions uniquely determine ind-pro-algebraic groups of interest
together with their derived categorical information.
   We need to treat functors in F ′ étale locally in derived categories. This treatment
is best done with Artin-Milne’s relative site constructions [AM76, §3, “an auxiliary
site X/Sperf ”]. Roughly speaking, we bundle the étale sites of A(F ′ ) for all F ′
together into a single site, so that it admits a morphism of sites to the perfect
artinian étale site. The cohomology groups as functors in F ′ are nothing but derived
pushforward sheaves. In Section 4, we develop a general theory of relative sites and
its compact support cohomology and cup product formalism.
   With this machinery, we can mostly follow Saito’s theory. In Sections 5, 6 and
7, we first develop the local theory, refining Kato’s two-dimensional local class field
theory with ind-pro-algebraic group structures. With Saito’s computations [Sai86,
(4.11) (A), (B)] recalled in Section 8, the case of “enough regular” A is done in
Section 10.3. For the general A, we take a resolution of singularities of A just as
Saito does. An additional local theory shows up from generic points of the special
fiber (or the reduced exceptional divisor) of the resolution. This local theory is
treated in Section 9.

1.5. Technical difficulties. Our duality pairings are hard to calculate since they
are genuinely derived (as can be seen from the shift Z/pZ[1] above) and we have
to take care of higher Ext groups. To overcome this, the key idea is to change the
topology. If we do not work étale locally but instead Zariski locally, then higher
Ext groups vanish in the situations at hand, and we can explicitly calculate duality
pairings using Galois symbols and differential forms. We still need to ensure that
passage from the perfect artinian étale site to its Zariski topology version does not
6                                    TAKASHI SUZUKI


lose much information. This requires a detailed analysis of Eilenberg-Mac Lane’s
cubical construction, which is done in Section 3.
    In Saito’s theory, the additional local theory coming from generic points of the
special fiber of the resolution is Kato’s class field theory [Kat82] for local fields
with residue field a one-dimensional function field. Unfortunately, this does not
translate nicely to our setting. The reason is that a function field over F is not an
object functorial in (transcendental) extensions F ′ of F : if k is such a function field,
then k ⊗F F ′ is not necessarily a field. What works instead is that for a smooth
affine curve V with function field k, the object V ×F F ′ is again a smooth affine
curve (over F ′ ). This means that we need to develop a duality theory for p-adic
tubular neighborhoods of smooth affine curves. This is the most precise description
of Section 9.
    This local theory has two difficulties. First, we use p-adic nearby cycle con-
structions to reduce the study of duality for p-adic tubular neighborhoods to the
classical duality theory for smooth affine curves. But in the latter duality theory,
we have to use the version of compact support cohomology with local components
given by cohomology of complete local fields rather than henselian local fields (since
henselian local fields of positive characteristic do not satisfy a duality). We have
already encountered this type of compact support cohomology in [Suz20a], and it
is very complicated and does not work well with relative site constructions. For
example, if one considers the additive algebraic group Ga as a sheaf on the (small!)
étale site of V and pulls it back to a complete local field k̂x , the result is no longer
Ga . We deal with this problem by considering the morphism of sites
                                  G
                                              Spec k̂x,et → Vet
                         x a boundary point of V

(or its relative site version) as a fibered site over the poset {• ≤ •} and taking its
total site as the main setting of compact support cohomology and cup product for
tubular neighborhoods. The generalities on this setting are explained in Section
4.5. This difficulty also affects the step on resolution of singularities, where we need
fibered sites over more complicated posets. Hence we need more on fibered sites in
Section 4.6 and more on completions in Section 10.5.
   The second difficulty is that the calculations of the duality pairing for cohomology
of p-adic tubular neighborhoods of affine curves is hard and necessarily indirect since
affine curves are not local objects. We first need to take the nearby cycle functor
to the affine curve, work Nisnevich locally over the curve to write the pairing, and
then push it down to the base field. This step is explained in Sections 9.3 and 9.4.
Acknowledgments. The author would like to thank Kazuya Kato for his suggestion
of this project.

                   2. Notation and general constructions
   Throughout this paper, let F be a perfect field of characteristic p > 0. All
algebraic groups and group schemes are assumed commutative. The symbol ⊂
includes the case of equality.

2.1. Categories. The categories of sets and abelian groups are denoted by Set
and Ab, respectively. For an abelian category A, the category of complexes in A
in cohomological grading is denoted by Ch(A). If A → B is a morphism in Ch(A),
                     ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                             7


then its mapping cone is denoted by [A → B]. The homotopy category of Ch(A)
is denoted by K(A) with derived category D(A). The full subcategory of Ch(A)
consisting of bounded below, bounded above and bounded complexes are denoted
by Ch+ (A), Ch− (A) and Chb (A), respectively. Similar notation applies to K(A)
and D(A). If we say A → B → C is a distinguished triangle in a triangulated
category, we implicitly assume that a morphism C → A[1] to the shift of A is given,
and the triangle A → B → C → A[1] is distinguished.
2.2. Sites and derived categories. A good reference for these is [KS06]. All
sites in this paper are defined by given pretopologies. For a site S, we mean by
X ∈ S an object of the underlying category of S and by Y → X in S a morphism
in the underlying category of S. The categories of sheaves of sets and abelian
groups on S are denoted by Set(S) and Ab(S), respectively. Denote Ch(Ab(S))
                                                                    +
by Ch(S). Similar notation defines K(S) and D(S). Let Dtor            (S) ⊂ D+ (S) be
the full subcategory consisting of objects whose cohomology sheaves are torsion.
The sheaf-Hom functor for Ab(S) is denoted by HomS and its n-th right derived
functor by ExtnS . Denote ⊗ = ⊗Z and ⊗L = ⊗L       Z . For objects G, H, K ∈ D(S), we
say that a morphism G⊗L H → K is a perfect pairing if the two induced morphisms
G → R HomS (H, K) and H → R HomS (G, K) are both isomorphisms.
   A premorphism of sites f : S ′ → S between sites defined by pretopologies is a
functor f −1 from the underlying category of S to the underlying category of S ′
that sends covering families to covering families such that the natural morphism
f −1 (Y ×X Z) → f −1 Y ×f −1 X f −1 Z is an isomorphism whenever Y → X appears in a
covering family. Its pushforward functor f∗ : Ab(S ′ ) → Ab(S) sends acyclic sheaves
to acyclic sheaves, where a sheaf G ∈ Ab(S) is said to be acyclic if H n (X, G) = 0 for
all X ∈ S and n ≥ 1. Acyclic sheaves calculate Rf∗ ([Suz20a, Proposition 2.4.2]).
The pullback functors for sheaves of sets and abelian groups are denoted by f ∗set
and f ∗ , respectively. Note that f ∗set and f ∗ are not necessarily exact functors.
The left derived functor Lf ∗ : D(S) → D(S ′ ) of f ∗ exists and is left adjoint to
Rf∗ : D(S ′ ) → D(S) ([Suz21, the paragraph after Definition 2.3]). An object G ∈
D(S) is said to be f -compatible ([Suz21, Definition 2.5 (a)]) if the natural morphism
L(f |X )∗ (G|X ) → (Lf ∗ G)|f −1 X is an isomorphism for all X ∈ S, where G|X ∈
D(S/X) is the restriction to the localization S/X and f |X : S ′ /f −1 X → S/X
is the premorphism induced on the localizations. The object G is said to be f -
acyclic ([Suz21, Definition 2.5 (b)]) if the natural morphism G → Rf∗ Lf ∗ G is an
isomorphism. If f ∗set is exact (that is, commutes with finite inverse limits), we say
that f is a morphism of sites. In this case, we simply write f ∗set = f ∗ .
   Let f : T → S be a morphism of sites defined by a functor f −1 on the underlying
categories. Recall from [Suz21, Proposition 2.4] (or [Sta21, Tags 0B6C, 0B6D])
that there exist canonical morphisms
                  Rf∗ R HomT (G, H) → R HomS (Rf∗ G, Rf∗ H),
(2.2.1)                  Rf∗ G ⊗L Rf∗ H → Rf∗ (G ⊗L H)
in D(S) functorial in G, H ∈ D(T ). The second morphism results from the first,
and the first morphism is the derived functor of the morphism
                       f∗ HomT (G, H) → HomS (f∗ G, f∗ H)
of functoriality of f∗ , which is a morphism in Ch(S) functorial in G, H ∈ Ch(T ).
Here we used the following fact (proved during [Suz21, Proposition 2.4]):
8                                        TAKASHI SUZUKI


Proposition 2.2.1. Let G, H ∈ Ch(T ). Assume that H is K-injective. Then the
complex f∗ HomT (G, H) represents Rf∗ R HomT (G, H).

2.3. The ind-rational and perfect aritinian étale sites. The perfection of an
F -algebra (resp. an F -scheme) is the direct (resp. inverse) limit along Frobenius
morphisms on it. A scheme Y ′ over an Fp -scheme Y is said to be relatively perfect
over Y if its relative Frobenius morphism Y ′ → Y ′(p) over Y is an isomorphism. For
a ring A complete with respect to an ideal I containing p and a relatively perfect
A/I-algebra B ′ , its Kato canonical lifting over A ([Kat82, §1, Definition 1]) is a
unique complete A-algebra A′ together with an isomorphism A′ /IA′ ∼                = B ′ over A/I
               ′  n ′                              n                   ′
such that A /I A is formally étale over A/I for all n. If B is flat over A/I and A
is noetherian, then it is characterized as a unique complete flat A-algebra together
with an isomorphism A′ /IA′ ∼        = B ′ over A/I ([Kat82, §1, Lemma 1] plus [Sta21,
Tag 0912]).

fields over F . An ind-rational F -algebra is a direct limit of a filtered direct sys-
tem consisting of rational F -algebras. Let F indrat be the category of ind-rational

2.4. Quasi-algebraic groups. A (commutative) quasi-algebraic group over F is
the perfection of a group scheme of finite type over F ([Ser60]). The category
of quasi-algebraic groups over F with F -group scheme morphisms is denoted by
Alg/F . The identity component and the group of components of G ∈ Alg/F are
denoted by G0 and π0 (G), respectively. By abuse of notation, Ga , Gm and Wn
(p-typical Witt vectors of length n) are the perfections of the corresponding group
schemes of finite type. We say that a quasi-algebraic group is unipotent if it is
the perfection of such a group. Let Algu /F ⊂ Alg/F be the full subcategory of
unipotent groups.
   The procategory of Alg/F is denoted by PAlg/F . We consider its full subcate-
gory P′ Alg/F consisting of objects isomorphic to filtered inverse systems with affine
transition morphisms. Its objects are schemes. The ind-category of P′ Alg/F is de-
noted by IP′ Alg/F . The ind-category IPAlgu /F of the pro-category of Algu /F is
a full subcategory of IP′ Alg/F . The functors G 7→ G0 , π0 (G) naturally extend to
                                                                    ∼
IP′ Alg/F . An object G ∈ IP′ Alg/F is said to be connected if G0 → G. The Yoneda
                                 ′                indrat         perar         perar
functor gives a functor from IP Alg/F to Ab(Fproet ), Ab(Fet ) or Ab(Fzar            ).
                      ′
The image of G ∈ IP Alg/F by this functor is denoted by the same symbol G by
abuse of notation.

2.6. Two-dimensional local rings. When given a two-dimensional noetherian
henselian normal local ring A with residue field F , we use the following notation.
Let m be the maximal ideal of A. Let X = Spec A \ {m}. Let P be the set of height
one prime ideals of A. For p ∈ P , let Ahp be the henselian local ring of A at p, Kph
its fraction field, κ(p) the residue field of Ahp , and Fp the residue field of κ(p) with
respect to the natural valuation.

3.2. The cubical construction and Mac Lane’s resolution. We recall the cu-
bical construction Q(G) and Mac Lane’s resolution M (G) of an abelian group G
([ML57]). See also [Lod98, Section 13.2 and Exercise E.13.2.1]. These construc-
tions are useful for explicitly describing Ext groups over sites in terms of cohomol-
ogy groups. The goal is to show that, in order to prove duality statements over
       perar                                          perar
Spec Fet     , it is enough to prove them over Spec Fzar    (Proposition 3.4.4).
                        n         n
   For n ≥ 0, set 2 = {0, 1} , whose elements are n-tuples (ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) of
numbers ε(1), . . . , ε(n) ∈ 2 = {0, 1}. Let Q′ (G) be the graded abelian group in
                                                          n
non-negative degrees whose n-th term is Q′n (G) = Z[G2 ], which is freely generated
                        ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                           13

                                                                                n+1
by functions t : 2n → G. For each 1 ≤ i ≤ n + 1 and t ∈ G2                            , define functions
                     n
Ri t, Si t, Pi t ∈ G2 by
          (Ri t)(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) = t(ε(1), . . . , ε(i − 1), 0, ε(i), . . . , ε(n)),
          (Si t)(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) = t(ε(1), . . . , ε(i − 1), 1, ε(i), . . . , ε(n)),
          (Pi t)(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) = t(ε(1), . . . , ε(i − 1), 0, ε(i), . . . , ε(n))
                                            + t(ε(1), . . . , ε(i − 1), 1, ε(i), . . . , ε(n)),
                                                                               n
where the sum is the group operation in G. Define ∂t ∈ Z[G2 ] by
                                      n+1
                                      X
                               ∂t =         (−1)i (Pi t − Ri t − Si t),
                                      i=1
                                                                     n                        n
where the right-hand side is a (formal) sum in Z[G2 ] (not sums in G2 ). Then
∂ ◦ ∂ = 0. We consider Q′ (G) as a complex (in the homological grading) with
differential ∂.
                                           n
   For each 1 ≤ i ≤ n, a function t ∈ G2 is said to be an i-slab if t(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) =
0 whenever ε(i) = 0 or if t(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) = 0 whenever ε(i) = 1. For each
                                                      n
1 ≤ i ≤ n − 1 (where n ≥ 1), a function t ∈ G2 is said to be an i-diagonal if
t(ε(1), . . . , ε(n)) = 0 whenever ε(i) 6= ε(i + 1). Let NG ⊂ Q′ (G) be the graded
subgroup generated by all slabs and diagonals. It is a subcomplex. Each term
NG,n ⊂ Q′n (G) is a direct summand and the splitting Q′n (G) ∼       = NG,n ⊕ Qn (G)
can be taken functorially in G ([Pir96, Section 5], [JP91, Proposition 2.6]). This
splitting does not respect ∂, though.
   Now define a complex by Q(G) = Q′ (G)/NG . It has a G-augmentation given
by Q0 (G) = Z[G]/Z · (0) ։ G, (g) 7→ g. In particular, we have a Z-augmented
                             m             n                            m+n
complex Q(Z). For t ∈ G2 and u ∈ Z2 , define a function tu ∈ G2               by
     (tu)(ε(1), . . . , ε(m + n)) = t(ε(1), . . . , ε(m)) · u(ε(m + 1), . . . , ε(m + n)),
where the dot on the right-hand side is the Z-action on the abelian group G. The
linear extension of this operation defines a morphism Q(G) ⊗Z Q(Z) → Q(G) of
graded abelian groups. When G = Z, this gives a differential graded ring structure
on Q(Z), and when G is arbitrary, it gives a differential graded right Q(Z)-module
structure on Q(G). The augmentation Q(Z) ։ Z is a morphism of differential
graded rings when Z is viewed as concentrated in degree zero. In particular, Z can
be viewed as a differential graded left Q(Z)-module.
   Define M (G) = (· · · → M1 (G) → M0 (G)) to be the two-sided bar construc-
tion B(Q(G), Q(Z), Z) ([GM74, Appendix A]; the notation in [ML57, Section 7] is
Q(G) ⊗Q(Z) B(Q(Z), ηQ )). We do not review the definition of two-sided bar con-
structions. It is a G-augmented complex. As a graded abelian group (forgetting the
differential), the complex B(Q(G), Q(Z), Z) as a functor in G is given by Q(G)⊗Z B
for some graded abelian group B that does not depend on G. (The group B is given
by B̄(0, Q(Z), ηQ ) in the notation of [ML57, (7-5)] and B(Z, Q(Z), Z) as a two-sided
bar construction.) Each term Bn of B is free. The augmentation M (G) → G gives
a resolution · · · → M1 (G) → M0 (G) → G → 0 of G.
   The above constructions are functorial in G. Hence they extend to any sheaves
over any sites. That is, for any sheaf G ∈ Ab(S) on a site S, we have sheaves
and complexes of sheaves Z[G], Q′ (G), Q(G), M (G) in Ab(S) by the sheafifications
14                                 TAKASHI SUZUKI


of the presheaves X 7→ Z[G(X)], Q′ (G(X)), Q(G(X)), M (G(X)), respectively. The
complex M (G) is a resolution of G since sheafification is exact.
3.3. The underlying set complexes. We need to describe R HomFZar          perar from a

connected group G to a constant group H in order to prove Proposition 3.4.4 in the
next subsection. We take Mac Lane’s resolution of G. When describing R HomFZar     perar

                                                                                  2n
from M (G) to H, what matter are only the underlying sets of self products G of
G and various maps between them. Thus the problem is to describe the “underlying
set version” of M (G) or, better, of Q(G).
    Let Spec FZar be the category of F -algebras (or F -schemes) endowed with the
Zariski topology. Let Set be the topos of sets. The functor sending an F -scheme
X to its underlying set |X| defines a premorphism of sites f : Set → Spec FZar .
The pullback f ∗ Z[X] of the free abelian sheaf generated by a representable sheaf
X is given by the free abelian group Z[|X|] generated by |X|. For a (commutative)
group scheme G over F , define |Q′ |(G) = f ∗ Q′ (G), |Q|(G) = f ∗ Q(G) and |M |(G) =
f ∗ M (G). They are complexes of abelian groups. The n-th term |Q′ |n (G) of the
                                    n
complex |Q′ |(G) is given by Z[|G2 |]. Here is the key technical result:
Proposition 3.3.1. Let G be an integral group scheme over F . Then |Q|(G) is an
exact complex.
   This in particular claims that ∂ : |Q|1 (G) → |Q|0 (G) is surjective (no augmenta-
tion considered).
   Below we will prove the proposition. Here is the idea of the proof. Below we
identify a point of a scheme and the corresponding irreducible closed subset or
the integral closed subscheme. For an irreducible closed X ⊂ G, consider V ′ (X) :=
G×F X ⊂ G2 as an element of |Q′ |1 (G). Since the group operation map G×G → G
restricts to a surjection G × X ։ G, we have P1 (X) = G. Therefore
              ∂V ′ (X) = R1 (X) + S1 (X) − P1 (X) = G + X − G = X.
Thus any element of |Q′0 |(G) is a boundary. For any n ≥ 0 and irreducible closed

3.5. Equivariant structures with respect to base change. When a sheaf on
a site is base-changed to the localization at some object, it naturally acquires an
equivariant structure (or a descent datum). In this subsection, we give some basics
about this topic, paying attention to functoriality with respect to premorphisms of
sites without exact pullback functors. This is needed to understand the behavior
of the functor á under base change and the associated equivariant structures. The
proof of Statement (2) of Theorem 1.3.1 will be a straightforward consequence of
the results of this subsection.
   Let S be a site and Σ a group. Assume an action of Σ on the underlying category
of S is given. This means that for any σ ∈ Σ, we are given an equivalence of
                     ∼                                                             ∼
categories σ −1 : S → S, X 7→ σ −1 X, such that the composite τ −1 σ −1 X : S → S is
                 −1                           −1
naturally (στ ) . Assume moreover that σ for any σ preserves covering families.
                              ∼
The equivalence of sites S → S induced by the functor σ −1 is denoted by σ (so
                    −1
(σ∗ C)(X) = C(σ X) for a sheaf C and X ∈ S).
   Let SΣ be the category where the objectsFare the objects of S and the set of
morphisms HomSΣ (X, Y ) for objects X, Y is σ∈Σ HomS (X, σ −1 Y ). A morphism
is denoted by (f, σ), where σ ∈ Σ and f : X → σ −1 Y in S. The composite of
(f, σ) : X → Y and (g, τ ) : Y → Z is ((σ −1 g) ◦ f, τ σ). A family of morphisms
{(fλ , σλ ) : Xλ → X} with fixed target is said to be a covering if {σλ fλ : σλ Xλ → X}
is a covering in S (where σλ is the inverse of σλ−1 ). This defines a pretopology on
SΣ , and we consider SΣ as a site with this pretopology.
   The functor from the underlying category of S to the underlying category of SΣ
given by sending X to X and f : X → Y to (f, id) : X → Y defines a premorphism
of sites
(3.5.1)                             η Σ : SΣ → S.
The pushforward functor η∗Σ is called the forgetful functor and denoted by ForΣ .
Any sheaf on SΣ thus defines a sheaf on S. Note that (id, σ) : σ −1 X → X is an
isomorphism for any X ∈ S and σ ∈ Σ. Hence any sheaf on SΣ can be identified with
                                                                                ∼
its forgetful image C ∈ Set(S) together with a Σ-equivariant structure ϕσ : C →
σ∗ C (satisfying (σ∗ ϕτ ) ◦ ϕσ = ϕστ ). Thus Set(SΣ ) can be identified with the
category of Σ-equivariant sheaves on S. Here are basic properties of the forgetful
functor:
Proposition 3.5.1. The forgetful functor ForΣ : Ab(SΣ ) → Ab(S) on abelian
sheaves
     Lis exact and conservative. It sends the free abelian sheaf Z[X] on an object
X to σ∈Σ Z[σ −1 X].

                                                     F

tinuous and by [Sta21, Tag 00XM]. This gives the statement about Z[X].          

              4. Relative sites and cup product with support
  Let Sch be the category of schemes.
4.1. Relative sites. The following defines something like “X → Spec F ” when X
is not really an F -scheme or, even if X is an F -scheme, when we want the base
change X ×F F ′ to involve completions:
Definition 4.1.1.
   (1) An F perar -scheme is a functor X from the opposite of F perar to Sch satis-
       fying the following two properties:
        (a) X commutes with finite coproducts.
        (b) For any étale (resp. faithfully flat étale) morphism F ′ → F ′′ in F perar ,
            the morphism X(F ′′ ) → X(F ′ ) is finite étale (resp. finite faithfully flat
                                                       ∼
            étale), and we have X(F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ ) → X(F ′′ ) ×X(F ′ ) X(F ′′′ ) for any
                                 ′      ′′′    perar
            other morphism F → F in F                .
       If X(F ′ ) = Spec R(F ′ ) is affine for all F ′ ∈ F perar , the functor R is called
       an F perar -algebra.
   (2) A morphism of F perar -schemes is a natural transformation Y → X of
                                     ∼
       functors such that Y(F ′′ ) → Y(F ′ ) ×X(F ′ ) X(F ′′ ) for all étale morphisms
         ′       ′′     perar
       F → F in F             . A morphism of F perar -algebras R → S is similarly
       defined.
   The condition (1b) ensures that even though a scheme morphism X(F ) → Spec F
does not necessarily exist or X(F ′ ) is not necessarily X(F ) ×F F ′ , there is a certain
                              perar              perar
morphism of sites to Spec Fet        and Spec Fzar      (Proposition 4.1.10). We now
define an “étale site of X”.
Definition 4.1.2. An étale X-scheme is a pair (X ′ , F ′ ), where F ′ ∈ F perar and X ′
is an étale X(F ′ )-scheme. A morphism (X ′′ , F ′′ ) → (X ′ , F ′ ) of étale X-schemes
consists of an F -algebra homomorphism F ′ → F ′′ and a scheme morphism X ′′ →
X ′ such that the diagram
                                 X ′′ −−→ X ′
                                               
                                               
                                  y             y
                              X(F ′′ ) −−→ X(F ′ )
commutes. Composition is defined in the obvious way.
26                                   TAKASHI SUZUKI


   If X(F ′ ) is an affine scheme, Spec R(F ′ ), for all F ′ ∈ F perar , then we define
the category of étale R-algebras to be the opposite of the full subcategory of the
category of étale X-schemes (X ′ , F ′ ) with X ′ affine. Its objects are pairs (R′ , F ′ )
with F ′ ∈ F perar and R′ an étale R(F ′ )-algebra.
   For a scheme X, let Xτ be either the small étale site Xet (for τ = et) or the
                                                                       perar
small Nisnevich site Xnis (for τ = nis). Let Spec Fτperar be Spec Fet        if τ = et and
       perar
Spec Fzar if τ = nis.

Definition 4.1.3.
     (1) A family of morphisms {(Xλ′ , Fλ′ ) → (X ′ , F ′ )} of étale X-schemes with fixed
         target is said to be a τ -covering if each F ′ → Fλ′ is étale and {Xλ′ → X ′ }
         is a covering for the site Xτ′ .
     (2) This class of families defines a pretopology. Define Xτ to be the category of
         étale X-schemes endowed with the topology generated by this pretopology.

    Note that {F ′ → Fλ′ } is not required to be a covering. The topology of Xτ
is really about the X ′ components and not the F ′ components. This is needed
for Propositions 4.1.5 and 4.1.6. Our default choice of the topology is et. The
Nisnevich topology is used only in Section 9 in an auxiliary manner.
    The covering families for Xτ are generated by families {(Xλ′ , F ′ ) → (X ′ , F ′ )}
with {Xλ′ → X ′ } a τ -covering and singleton families {(X ′′ , F ′′ ) → (X ′′ , F ′ )} with
F ′ → F ′′ étale.
    Define a morphism of sites
                                      ε : Xet → Xnis
by the identity functor on the underlying categories.

Example 4.1.4. We give some examples of sheaves on Xτ . Let A1 be the sheaf of
rings on Xτ sending (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ Γ(X ′ , OX ′ ). Let Ga ∈ Ab(Xτ ) be the underlying
additive group of A1 . Let Gm ∈ Ab(Xτ ) be (A1 )× . For a quasi-coherent sheaf
M on X(F ), the sheaf (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ Γ(X ′ , M ) (where M is pulled back to X ′ as an
OX ′ -module) is denoted the same symbol M . For q ≥ 0, let Ωr ∈ Ab(Xτ ) be the
sheaf (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ Γ(X ′ , ΩrX ′ /Z ). The differential maps d : ΩrX ′ /Z → Ωr+1
                                                                                X ′ /Z form a
                                r         r+1
morphism of sheaves d : Ω → Ω . Since étale and Nisnevich cohomology with
coefficients in a quasi-coherent M and ΩX ′ /Z agree (both isomorphic to Zariski
cohomology), we have Rq ε∗ M = Rq ε∗ Ωr = 0 for all q ≥ 1. Let BΩr ⊂ ZΩr ⊂ Ωr
be the image and the kernel of d, respectively.
   Assume that X(F ′ ) is regular of characteristic p for all F ′ ∈ F perar . Then νn (r)
can be viewed as a sheaf (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ Γ(X ′ , νn (r)) on Xτ (where the right-hand
                                    ′
side is the sheaf νn (r) on Xet       ). The Cartier operator gives a morphism of sheaves
        r        r
C : ZΩ → Ω such that ν(r) := ν1 (r) = Ker(C − 1). If τ = et, the morphism C − 1
is surjective. If τ = nis, we set ξ(r) = Coker(C − 1) and ξ = ξ(0).
   Assume that X(F ′ ) is regular of dimension ≤ 1 such that X(F ′ )×Spec Z Spec Z[1/p]
is dense for all F ′ . Then Tn (r) can be viewed as a sheaf (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ Γ(X ′ , Tn (r))
on Xτ .

Proposition 4.1.5. Let (X ′′ , F ′′ ) be an étale X-scheme. Let F ′ → F ′′ be an étale
morphism in F perar . Then the sheafification of the morphism (X ′′ , F ′′ ) → (X ′′ , F ′ )
of representable presheaves of sets in Xτ is an isomorphism.
                         ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                 27


Proof. Let C ∈ Set(Xτ ) be any sheaf. Since (X ′′ , F ′′ ) → (X ′′ , F ′ ) is a τ -covering,
the sequence
                C(X ′′ , F ′ ) → C(X ′′ , F ′′ ) ⇒ C(X ′′ , F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′ )
is an equalizer. Since F ′ is étale over F ′′ , the multiplication map F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′ ։ F ′′
is a projection onto a direct factor. In particular, the morphism (X ′′ , F ′′ ) →
(X ′′ , F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′ ) is a τ -covering. Hence the induced map C(X ′′ , F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′ ) →
C(X ′′ , F ′′ ) is injective. With this and the above sequence, we know that the map
C(X ′′ , F ′ ) → C(X ′′ , F ′′ ) is bijective. As C is arbitrary, this implies the result. 
   The cohomology theory of Xτ is the cohomology theories of X(F ′ )τ bundled
together:
Proposition 4.1.6. Let (X ′ , F ′ ) be an étale X-scheme. Let Xτ /(X ′ , F ′ ) be the
localization of Xτ at (X ′ , F ′ ). Consider the functor X1′ 7→ (X1′ , F ′ ) from the cat-
egory of étale X ′ -schemes to the category of étale X-schemes over (X ′ , F ′ ). This
defines a morphism of sites
                                  h(X ′ ,F ′ ) : Xτ /(X ′ , F ′ ) → Xτ′ .
Its pushforward functor is exact.
Proof. First, the category of étale X ′ -schemes has finite inverse limits and the
functor X1′ 7→ (X1′ , F ′ ) commutes with these limits. Hence this functor defines
a morphism of sites (that is, its pullback functor is exact). Let C → D be an
epimorphism of sheaves of sets on Xτ /(X ′ , F ′ ). Let X1′ be an étale X ′ -scheme
and let x ∈ (h(X ′ ,F ′ ),∗ D)(X1′ ) = D(X1′ , F ′ ) be an arbitrary element. There exist
a τ -covering {(Xλ′ , Fλ′ ) → (X1′ , F ′ )}λ and elements xλ ∈ C(Xλ′ , Fλ′ ) such that the
images of x and xλ in D(Xλ′ , Fλ′ ) agree. By Proposition 4.1.5, we may take Fλ′ = F ′
for all λ. Then xλ ∈ (h(X ′ ,F ′ ),∗ D)(Xλ′ ). As {Xλ′ → X1′ }λ is a τ -covering, this shows
that f∗ C → h(X ′ ,F ′ ),∗ D is an epimorphism.                                           
Definition 4.1.7. In the notation of Proposition 4.1.6, for a sheaf C on Xτ (of
sets or abelian groups), we call h(X ′ ,F ′ ),∗ (C|(X ′ ,F ′ ) ) the restriction of C to Xτ′ and
denote it by C|Xτ′ .
   If X(F ′ ) = Spec R(F ′ ) is affine for all F ′ and X ′ = Spec R′ is affine, then C|Xτ′
is also denoted by C|R′τ . The restrictions as defined above determine the sheaf C:

     The topos-theoretic points of Xτ are described by those of X(F ′ )τ :
Proposition 4.1.9. Let F ′ ∈ F perar be a field. Let x′ be a point of X(F ′ )τ (that
is, the Spec of the henselian or strict henselian local ring of X(F ′ ) at a point if
τ = nis or τ = et, respectively).
      (1) For a sheaf C ∈ Set(Xτ ), consider the stalk Cx′ := (C|X(F ′ )τ )x′ of C|X(F ′ )τ ∈
          Set(X(F ′ )τ ) at x′ . The functor C 7→ Cx′ gives a morphism of topoi
          p(x′ ,F ′ ) : Set → Set(Xτ ), which is a topos-theoretic point of Set(Xτ ).
      (2) The family of points {p(x′ ,F ′ ) } over all such pairs (x′ , F ′ ) is conservative.
Proof. The functor C 7→ C|X(F ′ )τ commutes with all (small) direct and inverse
limits. Hence the functor C 7→ Cx′ commutes with all direct limits and all finite in-
verse limits. Therefore it gives a point. The conservativity follows from Proposition
4.1.8.                                                                              

   Now we define the “structure morphism” for Xτ . Its relative duality theory, in
the case of two-dimensional local rings, will be the main theorem of this paper.
Proposition 4.1.10. The (contravariant) functor F ′ 7→ (X(F ′ ), F ′ ) defines a mor-
phism of sites
                          πX,τ : Xτ → Spec Fτperar .
Proof. By assumption on X, the stated functor defines a premorphism of sites. The
functor has a left adjoint given by (X ′ , F ′ ) 7→ F ′ (as a functor from the category of
étale X-schemes to the opposite of F perar ). Hence its pullback functor is exact. 

     In particular, we have its right derived pushforward functor
                                 RπX,τ,∗ : D(Xτ ) → D(Fτperar ).
Our default choice of τ is et, so πX,et is also simply denoted by πX . If X(F ′ ) =
Spec R(F ′ ) is affine for all F ′ , then πX,τ is also denoted by πR,τ .
  We will see some functoriality of the above constructions.

4.2. Henselian neighborhoods. We give two kinds of base change results for
closed immersions. Let A be an F perar -algebra. Assume that A(F ′ ) is a finite
product of henselian local rings for all F ′ ∈ F perar and that A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ )
maps the Jacobson radical into the Jacobson radical for all morphisms F ′ → F ′′
in F perar . Let k(F ′ ) be the quotient of A(F ′ ) by the Jacobson radical (which is a
finite product of fields).
                            ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                               31


Proposition 4.2.1. The functor k is an F perar -algebra.
Proof. It obviously preserves finite products. Let F ′ → F ′′ be étale in F perar .
Since A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ ) is finite étale, the image of the Jacobson radical of A(F ′ )
generates the Jacobson radical of A(F ′′ ), and the induced map k(F ′ ) → k(F ′′ ) is
finite étale. Let F ′ → F ′′′ be another morphism in F perar . Then the natural map
A(F ′′ ) ⊗A(F ′ ) A(F ′′′ ) → A(F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ ) is an isomorphism. Since A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ )
is étale, the ideal of A(F ′′ ) ⊗A(F ′ ) A(F ′′′ ) generated by the image of the Jacobson
radical of A(F ′′′ ) contains the image of the Jacobson radical of A(F ′′ ). On the
other hand, the image of the Jacobson radical of A(F ′′′ ) in A(F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ ) generates
the Jacobson radical since F ′′′ → F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ is étale. Therefore, on the quotients,
                                    ∼
we have k(F ′′ ) ⊗k(F ′ ) k(F ′′′ ) → k(F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ ).                                

   The natural morphism A ։ k of F perar -algebras then defines a morphism of
sites
                     ik,τ := πk/A,τ : Spec kτ → Spec Aτ .
On the other hand, for an étale k-algebra (k ′ , F ′ ), the k(F ′ )-algebra k ′ is finite
étale, so it admits a canonical finite étale lifting to A(F ′ ). Denote this lifting by
A(k ′ , F ′ ).
Proposition 4.2.2. The functor (k ′ , F ′ ) 7→ (A(k ′ , F ′ ), F ′ ) defines a morphism of
sites
                        πA/k,τ : Spec Aτ → Spec kτ .
Proof. Let (k ′ , F ′ ) → (k ′′ , F ′′ ) be a morphism of étale k-algebras that appears in
a covering family for the site Spec kτ , that is, that both F ′ → F ′′ and k ′ → k ′′ are
étale. In the commutative diagram
                                        A(F ′ )     −−→       A(F ′′ )
                                                               
                                                               
                                         y                      y
                                      A(F ′ , k ′ ) −−→ A(F ′′ , k ′′ ),
all the morphisms but the lower horizontal one is finite étale. It follows that the
lower horizontal map is finite étale. Hence (A(F ′ , k ′ ), F ′ ) → (A(F ′′ , k ′′ ), F ′′ ) ap-
pears in a covering family for Spec Aτ .
    The quotient of the map A(k ′ , F ′ ) → A(k ′′ , F ′′ ) by the Jacobson radicals is
k → k ′′ . It follows that if k ′ → k ′′ is a τ -covering, then so is A(k ′ , F ′ ) → A(k ′′ , F ′′ ).
  ′

    Let (k ′ , F ′ ) → (k ′′′ , F ′′′ ) be another morphism of étale k-algebras. Then both
A(k ′′ , F ′′ )⊗A(k′ ,F ′ ) A(k ′′′ , F ′′′ ) and A(k ′′ ⊗k′ k ′′′ , F ′′ ⊗F ′ F ′′′ ) are finite étale liftings
of the finite étale k ′′′ -algebra k ′′ ⊗k′ k ′′′ to A(k ′′′ , F ′′′ ). Hence the natural morphism
between them is an isomorphism. Thus the stated functor defines a premorphism
of sites.
                         ∗set
    The pullback πA/k,τ         C of a sheaf C ∈ Set(kτ ) is given by the τ -sheafification
of the presheaf that sends an étale A-algebra (A′ , F ′ ) to the direct limit of the
sets C(k ′ , F ′ ), where the index category consists of an étale k(F ′ )-algebra k ′ and
an A(F ′ )-algebra homomorphism A(k ′ , F ′ ) → A′ . This index category is filtered.
                                                                          ∗set
Since filtered direct limits are exact, we know that πA/k,τ                       is exact. Thus πA/k,τ is
a morphism of sites.                                                                                           

4.3. Shriek functors and cup product. We develop a theory of compact support
cohomology and cup product for relative sites.
     Let X be an F perar -scheme. Set X = X(F ). Let i : Z ֒→ X be a closed im-
mersion. Set j : U = X \ Z ֒→ X. Then Z and U define F perar -schemes Z
and U, respectively, as seen after Proposition 4.1.13. The morphisms i and j
define natural morphisms i : Z → X and j : U → X of F perar -schemes, which
then define morphisms of sites i : Zet → Xet and j : Uet → Xet . For any F ′ ∈
F perar , we have the usual shriek functors i!F ′ : Ab(X(F ′ )et ) → Ab(Z(F ′ )et ) and
jF ′ ,! : Ab(U(F ′ )et ) → Ab(X(F ′ )et ) (see [Sta21, Tags 0F4Z and 0F59] for example).
For G ∈ Ab(X(F ′ )et ) and H ∈ Ab(U(F ′ )et ), by Proposition 4.1.8, the sheaves
i!F ′ (G|X(F ′ ) ) and jF ′ ,! (H|U(F ′ ) ) together with natural functoriality with respect to
varying F ′ ∈ F perar define objects of Ab(Zet ) and Ab(Xet ), respectively. We denote
them by i! G and j! H, respectively. The obtained functors i! : Ab(Xet ) → Ab(Zet )
and j! : Ab(Uet ) → Ab(Xet ) are right adjoint and left adjoint, respectively, to
i∗ : Ab(Zet ) → Ab(Xet ) and j ∗ : Ab(Xet ) → Ab(Uet ). The functor i! is left exact
and the functor j! is exact. We have distinguished triangles
(4.3.1)              i∗ Ri! G → G → Rj∗ j ∗ G,        j! j ∗ G → G → i∗ i∗ G

of the functors of additive categories with translation ([KS06, Definition 10.1.1])
                      [i∗ → i∗ j∗ j ∗ ][−1] : Ch(Xet ) → Ch(Zet ),
                      [j∗ → i∗ i∗ j∗ ][−1] : Ch(Uet ) → Ch(Xet ),
respectively. See [KS06, Sections 13.3, 14.3] for derived functors of functors of
additive categories with translation.

4.5. Fibered sites and cup product. We want a version of (4.4.4) where henselian
localizations are replaced by completions. But this is not obvious since the pull-
back functor for the morphism Ûx,et → Uet from the punctured completion does
not send acyclic sheaves to acyclic sheaves and hence we cannot naturally take
derived functors. We deal with this problem by considering the category of tuples
(G, (Gx )x∈Z , (ϕx )x∈Z ), where G is a sheaf on Uet , Gx is a sheaf on Ûx,et and ϕx is
                ∗
a morphism πÛ    /U
                     G → Gx . An injective resolution in this category consists of an
                x

injective resolution for G in Ab(Uet ) and an injective resolution for Gx in Ab(Ûx,et )
for each x with a compatibility between them, so that there is no problem in taking
derived functors. Actually the category of such tuples is the category of sheaves on
the “total site” of a certain fibered site. We will develop this machinery, especially
its compact support and cup product formalism.
   Let I be a finite poset (viewed as a category). Let {Si }i∈I = ({Si }i∈I , {fji }i≤j )
be a fibered site ([AGV72, Exposé VI, §7.2.1]). This means that Si for each i ∈ I
is a site and fji : Sj → Si for each j ≥ i is a morphism of sites such that fji ◦ fkj ∼ =
fki naturally (where we more precisely assume this on the level of the underlying
            −1    −1 ∼ −1
functors fkj   ◦ fji  = fki ). Assume for simplicity that Si contains an initial object
∅, the empty family { } is a covering of ∅ (so that the sheafification of ∅ is the initial
                                      −1
sheaf) and the underlying functor fji     of fji sends ∅ to ∅. (Note that we can always
add an initial object to a site without changing its topos.)
   We denote the total site of {Si }i∈I ([AGV72, Exposé VI, §Définition 7.4.1])
by (Si )i∈I . More explicitly, an object is a tuple ({Xi }i∈I , {ϕij }i≤j ), where Xi ∈
                −1
Si and ϕij : fji   Xi → Xj is a morphism in Sj satisfying the cocycle condition:
         −1
ϕjk ◦(fkj ϕij ) = ϕik for i ≤ j ≤ k. A morphism from ({Xi′ }, {ϕ′ij }) to ({Xi }, {ϕij })
                        ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                 39


is a set of morphisms Xi′ → Xi over i ∈ I that is compatible with {ϕ′ij } and {ϕij }.
A covering of an object ({Xi }, {ϕij }) is a family of morphisms ({Xλi }, {ϕλij }) →
({Xi }, {ϕij }) indexed by λ ∈ Λ such that the family Xλi → Xi indexed by λ is
a covering for all i. (In [AGV72, Exposé VI, §Définition 7.4.1], the total site is
defined with a slightly different underlying category, but the above site defines an
equivalent topos, which can be seen from the description below.)
    An object of the topos Set(Si )i∈I = Set((Si )i∈I ) consists of a tuple F = ({Fi }i∈I , {ϕij }i≤j ),
                                  ∗
where Fi ∈ Set(Si ) and ϕij : fji   Fi → Fj is a morphism in Set(Sj ) (or equivalently,
                                                                              ∗
ϕij : Fi → fji,∗ Fj in Set(Si )) satisfying the cocycle condition: ϕjk ◦ (fkj   ϕij ) = ϕik
                                         ′     ′
for i ≤ j ≤ k. A morphism from ({Fi }, {ϕij }) to ({Fi }, {ϕij }) is a set of morphisms
Fi′ → Fi over i ∈ I that is compatible with {ϕ′ij } and {ϕij }. We call Fi the i-th
projection of F .
    For each i′ ∈ I and Xi′ ∈ Si′ , consider the object qi−1   ′ Xi′ := ({Xi }, {ϕij }) of
                              −1               ′
(Si )i∈I defined by Xi = fii′ Xi for i ≥ i and Xi = ∅ else and ϕij is the natural

                  −1 −1       ∼   −1

                                    ′ Xi′ if i ≥ i and ∅ else.    Then this functor qi−1 ′

premorphism. For (Fi )i∈I ∈ Set(Si )i∈I , we have qi′ ,∗ (Fi )i∈I = Fi′ . Hence for
j ′ ≥ i′ , the ϕ part of the data gives a natural transformation
(4.5.1)              ϕij : qi′ ,∗ → fj ′ i′ ,∗ qj ′ ,∗ : Set(Si )i∈I → Set(Si′ ).
For Fi′ ∈ Set(Si′ ), the sheaf qi∗set
                                  ′    Fi′ = (Fi )i∈I is given by Fi = fii∗ ′ Fi′ if i ≥ i′ and

of I. (But it is not if i′ is not the minimum since qi∗set    ′   does not preserve the final

abelian sheaf pullback qi∗′ Gi′ = (Gi )i∈I is given by Gi = fii∗ ′ Gi′ if i ≥ i′ and Gi = 0
else. Hence qi∗′ is exact for any i′ , so qi′ ,∗ : Ab(Si )i∈I → Ab(Si′ ) sends (K-)injectives
to (K-)injectives and (K-)acyclics to (K-)acyclics.
   Here we already used the description of Ab(Si )i∈I as the (abelian) category of

Example 4.6.1. A toy example is the étale sites of the spectra of the F -algebra
homomorphisms
                                  F ((u))    ←−−      F [u± ]
                                     x                   x

This diagram is aimed at describing the cohomology of Spec F [[t]][u]/(ut) with sup-
port on the closed subscheme Spec F [u] (whose complement is Spec F ((t))) in terms
of the simpler pieces Spec F [u± ] (which is global and regular) and Spec F [[t, u]]/(ut)
(which is local) with gluing data Spec F ((u)). Actual examples we will see in Sec-
tions 10.4 and 10.5 will be more involved p-adic versions of this diagram.
   We want to describe πS,∗ and π̄S,! = [π̄S,∗ → π̄T,∗ ][−1] in terms of πTi . For i =

                  5. Setup for two-dimensional local fields
    Recall that F is a fixed perfect field of characteristic p > 0 throughout the
paper. Let k be a complete discrete valuation field with residue field F . Let K be
a henselian discrete valuation field of characteristic zero with residue field k. The
field k has either characteristic p and 0. Let K̂ be the completion of K. In this
section, we fix some notation for two-dimensional local fields, which will be used
in both Sections 6 (where k has characteristic p) and 7 (where k has characteristic
zero).


5.1. Relative étale sites for two-dimensional local fields. The ring of integers
Ok of k has a canonical structure as a complete W (F )-algebra, where W denotes
the ring of p-typical Witt vectors of infinite length. For a perfect artinian F -algebra
F ′ , define a k-algebra k(F ′ ) by

(5.1.1)                   k(F ′ ) = (W (F ′ ) ⊗
                                              ˆ W (F ) Ok ) ⊗Ok k.

The functor F ′ 7→ k(F ′ ) is an F perar -algebra. If F ′ is a field, then k(F ′ ) is a
complete discrete valuation field with residue field F ′ . There is a notion of lifting
of k to OK :

Definition 5.1.1. A lifting system for OK consists of:
     (1) a functor OK from the category of perfect artinian F -algebras to the cate-
         gory of OK -algebras, and
     (2) an isomorphism OK (F ′ ) ⊗OK k ∼
                                        = k(F ′ ) of k-algebras for any F ′ ∈ F perar
                        ′
         functorial in F ,
satisfying:
     (1) OK (F ′ ) is flat over OK ,
     (2) OK (F ′ ) is pK OK (F ′ )-adically separated and henselian, and
     (3) OK (F ′ ) → OK (F ′′ ) is finite if F ′ → F ′′ is étale.

    For a field F ′ ∈ F perar , the ring OK (F ′ ) is a henselian discrete valuation ring
with maximal ideal generated by a prime element of OK and with residue field F ′ .
    If k has characteristic p, there is the following canonical such data: For a perfect
field extension F ′ of F , if we write k ∼= F ((t)), then k(F ′ ) ∼= F ′ ((t)). In particular,
      ′                                                          ′
k(F ) is relatively perfect over k. The same is true if F is a perfect artinian F -
algebra. Define OK (F ′ ) to be the Kato canonical lifting of k(F ′ ) over ÔK . Then
this OK (F ′ ) with the natural reduction map gives a lifting system.
    Even if we start from a complete ring A as in Introduction, compact support
cohomology requires non-complete henselian local fields, which is why we need to
treat more general lifting systems than the canonical one.
    If k has characteristic zero, a lifting system (non-canonically) exists: by fixing a
continuous isomorphism ÔK ∼      = k[[̟K ]], one may take OK (F ′ ) = k(F ′ )[[̟K ]] with
the obvious isomorphism OK (F ′ ) ⊗OK k ∼      = k(F ′ ).
    In general, fix a choice of a lifting system for OK . Set K(F ′ ) = OK (F ′ ) ⊗OK K.
We have natural morphisms K ← OK → k of F perar -algebras. Applying them to
the constructions in Section 4, we obtain a commutative diagram of morphisms of

          6. Two-dimensional local fields of mixed characteristic
    Let k be a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic p with residue field
F . Let K be a henselian discrete valuation field of characteristic zero with residue
field k. In this section, we formulate and prove a duality for K and OK with
ind-pro-algebraic group structures on cohomology.
    Let Fr : k → k be the natural transformation given by the p-th power map on k.
                                    n         n
Set ℘ = Fr − 1. For n ≥ 1, let kp and k×p be the (presheaf) images of Frn on k
and k× , respectively. Let Ω1k be the functor F ′ 7→ Ω1k(F ′ ) . The Cartier operator on
                                                                                   perar

6.1. Review of known results. The main arithmetic inputs are Kato’s calcu-
lations of Galois cohomology and his duality pairings in terms of symbols and
differential forms. These calculations are classical, and we recall them here.
    Recall our notation ξn (F ) = Wn (F )/℘Wn (F ), where ℘ = Fr − 1, and ξ(F ) =
ξ1 (F ).
Proposition 6.1.1 ([Kat79, §5, Theorem 1]). Let n ≥ 1 and r ∈ Z. Then we have
H q (K, Λn (r)) = 0 for q ≥ 4. The boundary maps give morphisms
(6.1.1)          H 2 (K, Λn (2)) → H 1 (k, Λn (1)) → H 0 (F, Λn ) = Λn
and isomorphisms
                               ∼                 ∼

is isomorphic to H q−1 (k, νn (1)). The morphism (6.1.2) is compatible with the
morphism (6.1.1) in the following sense (see [Kat79, §5, Proof of Theorem 1, Step

F of F . Then the composite

that the residue field of k ′ is F . For F ′ ∈ F perar , let OK ′ (F ′ ) = OK (F ′ ) ⊗OK OK ′ .
Define Spec OK ′ ,et , Spec K′et , Spec k′et and so on similarly. Let f : Spec OK ′ ,et →
Spec OK,et be the natural morphism. Then we have canonical morphisms T(r) →
f∗ T(r) and f∗ T(r) → T(r) by [Sat07, Theorem 1.1.2]. The composite T(r) →
f∗ T(r) → T(r) is multiplication by [K ′ : K] by the projection formula [Sat07,
Corollary 7.2.4] and hence an isomorphism. Hence RπOK ,∗ T(r) and RπOK ,! T(r)
are direct summands of RπOK ′ ,∗ T(r) and RπOK ′ ,! T(r), respectively. Now the same
projection formula implies that the statement for OK ′ implies that for OK .
   The statement for general n follows from that for n = 1.                                
Theorem 6.5.3. Let n ≥ 1 and r, r′ ∈ Z with r + r′ = 2.
  (1) We have RΓ(OK , Λn (r)), RΓc (OK , Λn (r)), ∈ Db (IPAlgu /F ).
  (2) The morphism
                   RΓ(OK , Λn (r)) ⊗L RΓc (OK , Λn (r′ )) → Λ∞ [−3]
         obtained by applying á to the morphism (6.5.4) is a perfect pairing in

         D(Fproet    ).

Proof. This follows from Propositions 6.5.1, 3.1.5, 3.1.4 and 3.1.10.               

 7. Two-dimensional local fields with mixed characteristic residue
                                field
    Let k be a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic zero with residue
field F . Let K be a henselian discrete valuation field with residue field k. We will
give a duality for OK . We already have a duality for k by [Suz22b]. Since k has
characteristic zero, we can just apply Verdier duality to pass to OK . We still need
to make sure the duality statement can be stated in the manner parallel to the
duality in the previous section, so that these two duality statements can be applied
to the local fields of arbitrary height one primes of two-dimensional local rings in a
coherent manner. Since the statement for k in [Suz22b] treats slightly more general
coefficients than Λn (r), we can prove a slightly more general result than needed.
7.1. Nearby cycle duality. Let Dcb (ket ) ⊂ Db (ket ) be the full subcategory of
objects with constructible cohomology sheaves.
Proposition 7.1.1. Let N ∈ Dcb (ket ) and set M = R Homket (N, Q/Z(1)). View
them as objects of D(ket ) via pullback.
   (1) We have Rπk,∗ N ∈ Db (Alg/F ).
   (2) There exists a canonical morphism
                              Rπk,∗ Q/Z(1) → Q/Z[−1].
     (3) The composite morphism
                 Rπk,∗ N ⊗L Rπk,∗ M → Rπk,∗ Q/Z(1) → Q/Z[−1].
                                    perar
         is a perfect pairing in D(Fet    ).
                                                            rat                   perar
Proof. This follows from [Suz22b, Theorem A], with Spec Fet     replaced by Spec Fet
                           rat                   indrat
in the same way as Spec Fet    replaced by Spec Fet     in [Suz22b, Theorem 2.1.5].
                                                                                    
   Note that if N has order prime to p, then Rπk,∗ N is a complex of finite étale
group schemes over F .
   Let j0 : Spec Ket → Spec OK,et and i0 : Spec ket → Spec OK,et be the natu-
ral morphisms. Let N ∈ Dcb (OK,et ). Then i∗0 N, Ri!0 N ∈ Dcb (ket ). Set M =
R HomOK,et (N, Q/Z(2)). We have a canonical morphism Ri!0 Q/Z(2) ∼= Q/Z(1)[−2].
The composite morphism
                     i∗0 N ⊗L Ri!0 M → Ri!0 Q/Z(2) → Q/Z(1)[−2]
is a perfect pairing in D(ket ) by Verdier duality (see for example [Ill77, Exposé I,
Theorem 5.1]). Combining this with the above proposition, we get:
Proposition 7.1.2. Let N ∈ Dcb (OK,et ) and set M = R HomOK,et (N, Q/Z(2)).

8. Preliminary calculations on regular two-dimensional local rings
   Let A be a local ring with residue field F . As in [Sai86, §4], assume all of the
following:
     (1) A is regular, excellent, two-dimensional and henselian.
     (2) The fraction field K of A has characteristic zero.
     (3) A contains a primitive p-th root of unity ζp .
     (4) Either of the following holds:
          (a) (p) is divisible by exactly one prime ideal p and A/p is regular, or
          (b) (p) is divisible by exactly two prime ideals pα and pβ , and pα +pβ = m,
               and both A/pα and A/pβ are regular.
                                                                                     p
This is the situation we encounter after the embedded resolution of (Spec A, Spec A/ (p)).
In this section, we mostly recall the results of [Sai86, §4]. Basically we describe
H q (A[1/p], Λ) through embedding into H q (K, Λ) or H q (Kqh , Λ), where we have
Merkurjev-Suslin’s theorem [MS82]. This description will be used as a local theory
in Sections 9 and 10
   Set R = A[1/p]. For any q ≥ 0, let Kq be the q-th Quillen K-group functor and
K̄q the cokernel of multiplication by p on Kq . We use the notation of Section 2.6.
Let P ′ ⊂ P be the subset consisting of primes not dividing p (so it is P \ {p} in
Case (4a) and P \ {pα , pβ } in Case (4b)). In Case (4b), for ν = α or β, we write
Kνh = Kphν and Ahν = Ahpν .

8.1. First calculations.
Proposition 8.1.1. The map H q (R, Λ) → H q (K, Λ) is injective for all q. We
have H q (R, Λ) = H q (K, Λ) = 0 for all q ≥ 4. If F is algebraically closed, then
H q (R, Λ) = H q (K, Λ) = 0 for all q ≥ 3.
Proof. Consider the localization exact sequence (combined with purity)

Proposition 8.1.4. The natural map
                                              M
                              H q (R, Λ) →              H q (Kqh , Λ)
                                             q∈P \P ′

is injective for all q ≤ 2.
Proof. The injectivity is obvious for q = 0. For q = 1, an element of the kernel
corresponds to a finite étale covering of X completely decomposed at the points of
P \ P ′ . The purity of branch locus ([Sta21, Tag 0BMA]) shows that such a covering
extends to a finite étale covering of Spec A. A finite étale covering of Spec A comes
from a finite extension of F . As it is completely decomposed at the points of P \ P ′ ,
it is trivial.
    For q = 2, first we have H 2 (R, Λ) ∼= H 2 (R, Gm )[p] since Pic(R) = 0. We have a
localization exact sequence
                                                    M
                  H 2 (X, Gm ) → H 2 (R, Gm ) →           Hq3 (Ahq , Gm ),
                                                        q∈P \P ′
                      ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                              65


where Hq∗ denotes cohomology with support on the closed point. We have
             H 2 (X, Gm )[p∞ ] = H 2 (A, Gm )[p∞ ] = H 2 (F, Gm )[p∞ ] = 0
by the regularity of A and the purity for Brauer groups ([Gro68, Theorem (6.1),
Corollary (6.2)]). The second map factors as
                            M                      M
            H 2 (R, Gm ) →       H 2 (Kqh , Gm ) →     Hq3 (Ahν , Gm ).
                             q∈P \P ′                        q∈P \P ′

Thus the map is injective.                                                              

   The injectivity holds for q ≥ 3 as well. We will see this in the next two subsec-
tions.

                9. Tubular neighborhoods of affine curves
   In this section, we first give a duality for cohomology of smooth affine curves
over F with coefficients in vector bundles, Λ and ν(1) (Section 9.1). This is more
or less the classical coherent duality, but we need to formulate it in the style of
fibered sites of Section 4.5. With this duality formulated in this style, we give a
duality for cohomology of p-adic tubular neighborhoods of smooth affine curves for
the rest of the section. We build relative and fibered sites associated with tubular
neighborhoods and define nearby cycle functors in this setting in Section 9.2. Then
we introduce filtrations on nearby cycles by symbols and prove a duality for the
zero-th graded piece (over the relative étale site of the curve) in Section 9.3. For
the other graded pieces, in Section 9.4, we work over the relative Nisnevich site of
the curve to prove the duality. In Section 9.5, we combine these two pieces of the
duality, yielding the desired duality for cohomology of tubular neighborhoods.

9.1. Relative sites for affine curves and duality. Let B be a smooth geomet-
rically connected F -algebra of dimension 1 with function field k. Set V = Spec B.

9.2. Relative sites for tubular neighborhoods. Let A be a ring. Assume all
of the following:
    (1) A is a two-dimensional regular integral domain.
    (2) A contains a primitive p-th root of unity ζp .
    (3) The radical I of the ideal (p) of A is principal.
    (4) B := A/I is a one-dimensional geometrically connected smooth algebra
         over F .
    (5) The pair (A, I) is complete.
                        ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                  75


This means that Spec A is a p-adic tubular neighborhood of the curve Spec B. The
conditions (2) and (3) are for simplicity. We will apply the results of this section to
two-dimensional local rings in the next section after enough reduction steps where
these conditions are satisfied. In particular, the coefficient sheaf for the duality
will be just Λ, not Λn (r). We do not try to give best general results for tubular
neighborhoods of their own.
    Note that A is excellent by [Gre82, Proposition 2.3] and [KS21, Main Theorem
1]. Let ̟ be a generator of I. Set R = A[1/p]. We use the same notation as
Section 9.1 applied to B. For example, let Y be the smooth compactification of
V := Spec B.

Remark 9.5.8. The duality theorem in this section should be closely related to the
duality for relatively perfect nearby cycles ([KS19]) in the following manner. Let k0
(∋ ζp ) be a mixed characteristic complete discrete valuation field with prime element
̟ and residue field F . Let A0 be a smooth algebra over Ok0 with relative dimension
1 and geometrically irreducible fibers. Then its ̟-adic completion A satisfies the
conditions listed at the beginning of Section 9.2. The duality theorem in [KS19]
gives a duality for the relatively perfect nearby cycle functor RΨRP : D(R0,Et ) →
D(BRPS ), where R0 = A0 [1/p] and B = A/̟A. This should be compatible with
the nearby cycle functor RΨ : D(R̂et ) → D(Bet ) of this paper in a suitable sense.
   This paper might be simplified and the results strengthened if we could just use
the result of [KS19]. This route does not seem possible at present, however, since
our A does not necessarily contain such a base Ok0 . Our theory is a fragment of
a hypothetical theory of p-adic nearby cycles “without a base”. It is hoped that
                                                                          j     i
there is such a theory applicable to the resolution of singularities X → X ← Y of
Section 10.4.
88                                    TAKASHI SUZUKI


                          10. Two-dimensional local rings
   Let A be an excellent henselian normal two-dimensional local ring of mixed
characteristic (0, p) with maximal ideal m and residue field F . We use the notation
of Section 2.6. Let Â be the completion of A.
                                                                       perar
   In Section 10.1, we first formulate the duality for A over Spec Fet       . Its proof
finishes at Section
                  p  10.6. For the proof, the case where  the embedded    resolution  of
(Spec A, Spec A/ (p)) is already done is treated in Section 10.3 using the results
of Section 8. In Section 10.4, we take a resolution of A and localize the cohomology
theory along the special fiber (or the reduced exceptional divisor) of the resolution.
This gives a fibered site of the type of Section 4.6 consisting of relative étale sites
of various henselian local rings and fields and henselian neighborhoods of smooth
affine curves over F . In Section 10.5, we take the completions of these local pieces.
The duality for these completed local pieces are proved by the duality results of
Sections 6, 9 and 10.3. In advance to that, we show the completion invariance of
the duality statement for A in an earlier Section 10.2. (It is necessary for A to be
complete for the duality to hold, but we may replace the henselian local fields at
height one primes by complete local fields.)

10.1. Setup. As explained in Introduction, we need to vary the residue field of A.
Even though there is a canonical way to do so (see Introduction or Definition 10.1.2
below), we need a slight more flexibility:
Definition 10.1.1. A lifting system for A consists of:
    (1) a functor A from F perar to the category of A-algebras, and
                                             ∼
    (2) an isomorphism A(F ′ )/mA(F ′ ) → F ′ of F -algebras functorial in F ′ ,
satisfying the conditions that:
    (1) A commutes with finite products,
    (2) A(F ′ ) is flat over A for all F ′ ∈ F perar ,
    (3) if F ′ ∈ F perar is a field, then A(F ′ ) is an excellent henselian normal two-
         dimensional local ring with maximal ideal mA(F ′ ), and
    (4) if F ′ → F ′′ is an étale morphism in F perar , then A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ ) is finite.
    Condition 2 implies that A(F ′ )/mn A(F ′ ) is the Kato canonical lifting of F ′ over
A/mn for any n. In particular, A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ ) is flat for all morphisms F ′ → F ′′
in F perar . In Condition 4, F ′ → F ′′ being étale implies it is finite étale, and
A(F ′ ) → A(F ′′ ) being finite implies it is finite étale. If F0 ∈ F perar is a field, then
the restriction of A to F0perar is a lifting system for A(F0 ). Such a non-complete
lifting shows up from resolutions of A in Section 10.4.
    The Teichmüller map gives a canonical W (F )-algebra structure on Â ([DG70,
Chapter V, Section 4, Theorem 2.1]).
Definition 10.1.2. The canonical lifting system for A is defined by
               Â(F ′ ) = W (F ′ )⊗
                                  ˆ W (F ) Â = lim(Wn (F ′ ) ⊗Wn (F ) (A/mn ))
                                                ←−
                                               n

for all F ′ ∈ F perar .
   This is indeed a lifting system for A. If F0 ∈ F perar is a field, then the restriction
of Â to F0perar is the canonical lifting system for Â(F0 ). Below we fix a lifting
system A for A. For any F ′ ∈ F perar , the completion of A(F ′ ) with respect to
                       ARITHMETIC DUALITY IN TWO-DIMENSION                                 89


mA(F ′ ) is canonically isomorphic to Â(F ′ ). In particular, we have a canonical
morphism A → Â of F perar -algebras.

10.4. Localization over resolution of singularities. We return to general A,
Â and S. We recall some of the notation in [Sai86, §1] with some slight additions
and modifications. Let πX/A : X → Spec A be a resolution of singularities such that
Y ∪ S ⊂ X is supported on a strict normal crossing divisor, where Y is the reduced
part of X ×A F ([Sta21, Tag 0BIC]). Let Y0 (resp. Y1 ) be the set of closed (resp.
generic) points of Y . For η ∈ Y1 , let η0 = {η} ∩ Y0 and Fη the constant field of
{η}. For x ∈ Y0 , let Ahx be the henselian local ring of X at x with maximal ideal
mAhx and residue field Fx and let Y1x be the set of height one primes of Ahx lying
over some element of Y1 (via the morphism Spec Ahx → X). For η ∈ Y1 and x ∈ η0 ,
there is a unique ηx ∈ Y1x lying over η. Let Kηhx be the henselian local field of Ahx
         h
at ηx , OK ηx

  h                                                        h
                                                                = O(Spec Ahx ×X US ).
   We will take henselian neighborhoods of small enough affine opens of irreducible
components of Y . For each η ∈ Y1 , choose an affine open neighborhood Wη ⊂ X of
η small enough so that:



        • Wη ∩ {η ′ } = ∅ for any η ′ ∈ Y1 \ {η},
        • Wη does not contain the specialization of any element of S in Y and
        • Wη ∩ Y ⊂ Wη is a principal divisor.

Proposition 10.5.6. The statement of Proposition 10.1.5 is true if n = 1, ζp ∈ A
and US = Spec A[1/p].
Proof. This follows from Propositions 10.5.2 and 10.5.5.                                      
10.6. Main theorem. Now we can finish the proof of Proposition 10.1.5.
Proof of Proposition 10.1.5. The case n ≥ 1 is reduced to the case n = 1. Let
S ′ ⊂ P be a finite subset containing S. Let US ′ = X \ S ′ . Then the statement
for US is equivalent to the statement for US ′ . Indeed, we have a morphism of
distinguished triangles

in D(Fet      ) by Proposition 4.3.2. The left vertical morphism is an isomorphism
of objects of hWF iFetperar by Propositions 6.5.1 and 7.2.3. Therefore the invertibility
of the other two vertical morphisms is equivalent to each other.
   Next, to prove the statement of the theorem, we may assume ζp ∈ A. Indeed,
let A′ be the normalization of A in K(ζp ). Let US′ ′ be the inverse image of US by
the morphism Spec A′ → Spec A. We may assume that S contains all primes above
p and US′ ′ → US is étale by the previous step. Since [K(ζp ) : K] is prime to p, a
norm argument shows that the statement for US′ ′ implies the statement for US .
106                                     TAKASHI SUZUKI


  Now we are reduced to the case n = 1, ζp ∈ A and US = Spec A[1/p], which is
proved in Proposition 10.5.6.                                              
Theorem 10.6.1. Assume that A is complete and take A to be the canonical lifting
system. Let n ≥ 1. Let r, r′ ∈ Z with r + r′ = 2.
    (1) The objects RΓ(US , Tn (r)) and RΓc (US , Tn (r)) belong to Db (IPAlgu /F ).
    (2) The composite morphism
         RΓ(US , Tn (r)) ⊗L RΓc (US , Tn (r′ )) → RΓc (US , Tn (2)) → Λ∞ [−3]
         obtained by applying á to the morphism in Proposition 10.1.5 (2) is a perfect
                        indrat
         pairing in D(Fproet   ).
Proof. This follows from Propositions 10.1.5, 3.1.5, 3.1.4 and 3.1.10.                           
   This finishes the proof of Theorems 1.3.1 and 1.3.2: Statement (1) of the first
theorem is Proposition 3.1.4; Statement (2) follows from Proposition 4.3.3; and
Statement (3) is a consequence of the previous two statements.
